## modern-en · marcus-aemilius-lepidus · 3:15

Another, contrary curiosity (and there is no shortage of examples in my own family) seems somehow akin to this one: that a man should rack his brains in his last moments to plan obsequies of so peculiarly studied a frugality as to have one servant with a lantern. I see this humour praised, and the arrangement of Marcus Aemilius Lepidus, who forbade his heirs to spend on his funeral even the common ceremonies usual on such occasions. Is it really temperance and frugality to avoid expense and pleasure when the use and knowledge of them is imperceptible to us? An easy and cheap reformation. If guidance were needed here, I would say that in this, as in every other action of life, each person ought to regulate the matter according to his fortune. The philosopher Lycon prudently told his friends to dispose of his body wherever they thought best, and as for the funeral, to make it neither too superfluous nor too mean. For my part, I would leave the ordering of this ceremony entirely to custom, and shall, when the time comes, leave it to the discretion of whoever has to do me that last office. "Totus hic locus est contemnendus in nobis, non negligendus in nostris"; and a saint said this, very holily: "Curatio funeris, conditio sepulturae: pompa exequiarum, magis sunt vivorum solatia, quam subsidia mortuorum." Which made Socrates answer Crito, who asked him at his death how he wished to be buried: "However you like." If I were to occupy myself beyond the present with this affair at all, I should be most tempted, as the highest pleasure of this kind, to imitate those who in their lifetime entertain themselves with the ceremony and honours of their own obsequies in advance, and take pleasure in looking at their own dead face in marble. Happy are they who can gratify their senses by insensibility, and live by their death.

Who forbade his heirs to spend on his hearse even the ceremonies customary at a funeral.

## modern-en · lycon · 3:15

Another, contrary curiosity (and there is no shortage of examples in my own family) seems somehow akin to this one: that a man should rack his brains in his last moments to plan obsequies of so peculiarly studied a frugality as to have one servant with a lantern. I see this humour praised, and the arrangement of Marcus Aemilius Lepidus, who forbade his heirs to spend on his funeral even the common ceremonies usual on such occasions. Is it really temperance and frugality to avoid expense and pleasure when the use and knowledge of them is imperceptible to us? An easy and cheap reformation. If guidance were needed here, I would say that in this, as in every other action of life, each person ought to regulate the matter according to his fortune. The philosopher Lycon prudently told his friends to dispose of his body wherever they thought best, and as for the funeral, to make it neither too superfluous nor too mean. For my part, I would leave the ordering of this ceremony entirely to custom, and shall, when the time comes, leave it to the discretion of whoever has to do me that last office. "Totus hic locus est contemnendus in nobis, non negligendus in nostris"; and a saint said this, very holily: "Curatio funeris, conditio sepulturae: pompa exequiarum, magis sunt vivorum solatia, quam subsidia mortuorum." Which made Socrates answer Crito, who asked him at his death how he wished to be buried: "However you like." Happy are they who can gratify their senses by insensibility, and live by their death.

The philosopher who told his friends to put his body where they thought fit and to make his funeral neither too superfluous nor too mean.

## modern-en · diomedon · 3:16

I am ready to feel an implacable hatred for every form of popular rule, though I think it the most natural and equitable of all, every time I remember the inhuman injustice of the people of Athens, who, without any hearing or vouchsafing the men a word in their defence, put to death their brave captains just returned in triumph from a naval victory they had won over the Lacedaemonians near the Arginusae Isles — the bloodiest and most stubborn engagement the Greeks ever fought at sea. The reason: that after the victory they had pressed their advantage according to the rule of war rather than stop to collect and bury their dead. The execution is the more odious for the behaviour of Diomedon, one of the condemned and a man of eminent virtue in both politics and arms. Once the sentence had been pronounced — no audience had been allowed until then — he stepped forward to speak, and instead of pleading his own cause or arguing the impiety of so cruel a sentence, only expressed concern for his judges' welfare. He asked the gods to turn this sentence to their good, and prayed that, for his and his companions' failure to fulfil the vows they had made (which he then named) in thanks for so glorious a victory, the gods' anger might not fall on them. And so without more words he went courageously to his death.

One of the Athenian captains condemned after the victory at the Arginusae for not staying to bury their dead. Allowed to speak at last, he said nothing of his own cause or of the cruelty of the sentence, but asked the gods to turn it to his judges’ good and prayed that their neglect of the vows he and his companions had made might not be visited on the city; and went to his death.

## modern-en · chabrias · 3:17

Fortune punished them in kind a few years later. Chabrias, captain-general of their naval forces, had the better of Pollis, the Spartan admiral, at the Isle of Naxos, but lost the whole fruit of his victory, a thing of great importance to their affairs, in order not to fall under this example: rather than lose a few bodies of dead friends that were floating in the sea, he gave a host of living enemies the chance to sail away in safety — and they made the Athenians pay dearly afterwards for this unseasonable superstition.

The Athenian captain-general who, beating Pollis of Sparta at Naxos, threw away the whole fruit of the victory rather than risk Diomedon’s fate, and stopped to pick up a few floating bodies while a world of living enemies sailed away.

The Latin poet of the Pharsalia, on the civil war between Caesar and Pompey.

## modern-en · plutarch · 4:3

— so it seems the soul, once carried away and unsettled, turns its violence upon itself if not supplied with something to resist it. It always needs an object to aim at and to work upon. Plutarch says of those who dote on little dogs and monkeys that the loving part in us, lacking a legitimate object rather than lie idle, forges and invents a false and frivolous one. And we can see that the soul, in its passions, would rather deceive itself by creating a false and fantastical subject — even one it does not really believe in — than have nothing at all to work upon. In the same way brute beasts direct their fury at the stone or weapon that has hurt them, and even take their revenge with their teeth on themselves for the injury they have received from another:

The Greek biographer and moralist, and the book Montaigne says he always has at hand. Most of the ancient examples in the Essays come through him, and Montaigne defends him by name against the charge of credulity.

## modern-en · bion · 4:9

It is a common reflex. And the philosopher Bion said wittily of the king who tore his hair out by the handful in grief: "Does this man think baldness is a remedy for sorrow?" Who has not seen peevish gamblers chewing and swallowing the cards or the dice in revenge for the loss of their money? Xerxes whipped the sea and sent a written challenge to Mount Athos; Cyrus kept a whole army at work for several days to take his revenge on the river Gyndes, for the fright it had given him in crossing; and Caligula tore down a beautiful palace because his mother had once enjoyed herself there.

The philosopher of the ready retort — it is he who asked of a king tearing his hair out for sorrow whether the man thought baldness a remedy for grief.

## modern-en · augustus · 4:10

I remember a story going around when I was a boy — that one of our neighbouring kings, having received a blow from the hand of God, swore he would have his revenge, and to that end issued a proclamation that for the next ten years no one should pray to Him, or so much as mention Him throughout his dominions, or, as far as his authority went, believe in Him; by which they meant to paint not so much the folly as the vainglory of the nation the tale was told about. The two vices always travel together, but in truth such actions have in them still more presumption than want of wit. Augustus Caesar, having been tossed about by a storm at sea, took to defying Neptune, and during the pomp of the Circensian games, by way of revenge, removed his statue from its place among the other deities. In which he was still less excusable than the man before him, and less so than he was again later when, having lost a battle under Quintilius Varus in Germany, in rage and despair he ran his head against the wall, crying out, "Varus, give me back my legions!" These outdo all folly, since impiety is now added to them — invading God himself, or at least Fortune, as if she had ears subject to our batteries; like the Thracians who, when it thunders or lightens, fall to shooting against heaven with Titan-like vengeance, as if by flights of arrows they meant to bring God to reason. Even though the ancient poet in Plutarch tells us —

The first Roman emperor, Julius Caesar’s heir. Among Montaigne’s examples of him: he defied Neptune after a storm at sea and had the god’s statue taken down from among the deities at the games, and after losing the legions under Quintilius Varus in Germany he ran his head against the wall crying “O Varus! give me back my legions!”

## modern-en · quintus-marcius · 5:0

Quintus Marcius, the Roman legate in the war against Perseus, King of Macedon, in order to gain time to reinforce his army, put out some openings for an accommodation, by which the king was lulled into agreeing to a truce of several days — long enough to give his enemy the opportunity and leisure to recruit fresh forces, which was the cause of the king's final ruin. Yet the elder senators, mindful of their forefathers' ways, condemned this procedure as a falling-off from their ancient practice, which, they said, was to fight by valour, not by trickery, surprises, or night-encounters; not by feigned flight or sudden rallies to overcome the enemy; and never to make war without first proclaiming it, often setting both the hour and the place of battle. It was on this generous principle that they handed Pyrrhus's treacherous physician back to him, and the Etruscans' disloyal schoolmaster back to them. This was a truly Roman procedure, with no resemblance to Greek subtlety or to Punic cunning, where it was reckoned a less glorious victory to overcome by force than by fraud. Deceit may serve in a pinch, but only that man can truly admit he has been beaten who knows he has been overcome neither by policy nor by accident, but by sheer valour, man to man, in fair and just war. It clearly appears from the talk of those good old senators that this fine line had not yet found its way among them:

The Roman legate against Perseus of Macedon, who opened sham overtures of accommodation to gain time to reinforce, and was condemned for it by the elder senators as degenerating from the Roman practice of fighting by valour and not by artifice.

## modern-en · polybius · 5:3

The Achaeans, says Polybius, abhorred every kind of double-dealing in war, and did not count it a victory unless the enemy's courage had been fairly subdued.

The Greek historian of Rome’s rise, cited for the Achaeans, who counted no victory won unless the enemy’s courage was fairly subdued.

## modern-en · lysander · 5:9

For our part, we are less scrupulous about such things, and grant the honour of war to whoever profits by it. We say, with Lysander, that "where the lion's skin is too short, we must eke it out with a piece of the fox's," and the most common openings for surprise come from this practice. There are no moments when a commander needs to be more on his guard than during parleys and negotiations for accommodation. From which it has become a general rule among the soldiers of recent times that the governor of a place under siege should never go out to parley. It was for breaking this rule that, in our fathers' day, the Seigneurs de Montmord and de l'Assigny, defending Mousson against the Count of Nassau, were so harshly criticised. Even so, it might be excusable in a governor who, when he goes out, does so in a way that keeps the safety and the advantage on his own side — as Count Guido di Rangone did at Reggio (if we believe Du Bellay; Guicciardini says it was Guicciardini himself) when the Seigneur de l'Escut came up to parley. He stepped so little away from his fort that, when a disturbance broke out during the parley, not only did Monsieur de l'Escut and his party — who had advanced with him — find themselves much the weaker (so much so that Alessandro Trivulzio was killed there), but l'Escut himself was glad to follow the count and, trusting to his honour, take cover behind the walls of the town from the danger of the shot.

The Spartan admiral, whose maxim Montaigne quotes for his own countrymen: where the lion’s skin is too short, eke it out with a piece of the fox’s.

It was for breaking this rule that, in our fathers' day, the Seigneurs de Montmord and de l'Assigny, defending Mousson against the Count of Nassau, were so harshly criticised. Even so, it might be excusable in a governor who, when he goes out, does so in a way that keeps the safety and the advantage on his own side — as Count Guido di Rangone did at Reggio (if we believe Du Bellay; Guicciardini says it was Guicciardini himself) when the Seigneur de l'Escut came up to parley. He stepped so little away from his fort that, when a disturbance broke out during the parley, not only did Monsieur de l'Escut and his party — who had advanced with him — find themselves much the weaker (so much so that Alessandro Trivulzio was killed there), but l'Escut himself was glad to follow the count and, trusting to his honour, take cover behind the walls of the town from the danger of the shot.

Who came up to parley at Reggio and found himself the weaker when the parley broke down, and relied on Rangone’s honour to get him within the walls.

## modern-en · martin-du-bellay · 5:9

For our part, we are less scrupulous about such things, and grant the honour of war to whoever profits by it. We say, with Lysander, that "where the lion's skin is too short, we must eke it out with a piece of the fox's," and the most common openings for surprise come from this practice. There are no moments when a commander needs to be more on his guard than during parleys and negotiations for accommodation. From which it has become a general rule among the soldiers of recent times that the governor of a place under siege should never go out to parley. He stepped so little away from his fort that, when a disturbance broke out during the parley, not only did Monsieur de l'Escut and his party — who had advanced with him — find themselves much the weaker (so much so that Alessandro Trivulzio was killed there), but l'Escut himself was glad to follow the count and, trusting to his honour, take cover behind the walls of the town from the danger of the shot.

The soldier and memoirist whose account of the French wars Montaigne uses and sometimes doubts. Not the Cardinal du Bellay, nor the poet.

## modern-en · eumenes · 5:10

Eumenes, shut up in the city of Nora by Antigonus and pressed by him to come out and speak with him (since, as Antigonus put it, he was the greater of the two and now had the advantage), returned this noble answer: "Tell him that I shall never reckon any man greater than myself while I have my sword in my hand," and he would not consent to come out until Antigonus had, at his own demand, delivered up his nephew Ptolomeus as a hostage.

The Greek secretary and general of Alexander’s successors, shut up in Nora by Antigonus. Summoned out on the ground that Antigonus was the greater man, he answered that he would never think any man greater than himself while he had his sword in his hand.

## modern-en · antigonus-i · 5:10

## modern-en · henry-de-vaux · 5:11

And yet some have done very well going out in person to parley, on the assailant's word — witness Henri de Vaux, a cavalier of Champagne, who, besieged by the English in the castle of Commercy, found that Bartholomew de Brunes, the besieging commander, had sapped so much of the castle from outside that nothing remained but to set fire to the props and bury the besieged under the ruins. Brunes asked Henri to come out and speak with him for his own good, which he did with three others in company. His destruction was made plain to him, and feeling himself singularly obliged to his enemy he and his garrison surrendered themselves to his discretion. As soon as the mine was fired and the props began to fail, the castle was instantly blown up from its foundations, not one stone left upon another.

The cavalier of Champagne besieged in the Castle of Commercy, who went out on the word of his enemy, was shown the mine that would have buried him, and thought himself so obliged that he surrendered himself and his garrison.

## modern-en · bartholomew-de-brunes · 5:11

## modern-en · lucius-aemilius-regillus · 6:1

Lucius Aemilius Regillus, the Roman praetor, having wasted his time trying to take the city of Phocaea by force because of the extraordinary courage with which the inhabitants defended themselves, agreed at last to receive them as friends of the people of Rome and to enter the town as he would enter a confederate city, with no kind of hostility — and gave them all the assurances they asked for. But once he had, for the greater pomp, brought his whole army in with him, it was no longer in his power, however hard he tried, to restrain his men. Avarice and revenge trampled both his authority and all military discipline underfoot, and he saw a considerable part of the city sacked and ruined before his eyes.

The Roman praetor who, unable to take Phocaea by force, received the town as a confederate with every assurance of peace — and then brought his whole army in for the pomp of it and could not stop them sacking it before his face.

## modern-en · cleomenes-i · 6:2

Cleomenes used to say that whatever harm a man could do his enemy in wartime was above justice and not accountable to it in the sight of gods or men. So, having concluded a seven-day truce with the Argives, he fell on them on the third night when they were all asleep and put them to the sword, alleging that no nights had been mentioned in the truce. But the gods punished this clever treachery.

The Spartan king who held that any mischief done an enemy in war was above justice, and who, having made a seven days’ truce with Argos, fell on the Argives asleep on the third night on the ground that nights had not been mentioned.

## modern-en · xenophon · 6:4

And undoubtedly war has by nature many privileges that look reasonable even when they go against reason. So here the rule fails — "Neminem id agere ut ex alterius praedetur inscitia." But I am astonished at the great liberty Xenophon allows in such cases, both by precept and by the example of his ideal commander's many exploits. He is an author of very great authority on such matters, I admit, being himself both a great captain and a leading philosopher among Socrates's disciples; and yet I cannot agree to the licence he grants in everything, in all places.

Socrates’s disciple and a soldier — both a great captain and a philosopher of the first form, as Montaigne puts it. His licence about stratagems in war is more than Montaigne will follow.

## modern-en · aubigny · 6:5

Monsieur d'Aubigny, besieging Capua and pounding it with a furious battery, found that Signor Fabrizio Colonna, the governor of the town, had begun a parley from a bastion. While his soldiers, meanwhile, slackened their guard, our men slipped in and put them all to the sword. And more recently, at Yvoy, Signor Giuliano Romero, playing the novice by going out to parley with the Constable, found his place taken at his return. But, that we should not get off scot-free, the Marquess of Pescara, laying siege to Genoa where Duke Ottaviano Fregoso commanded under our protection, had the terms between them so far advanced that the matter was looked on as done and on the point of being concluded — and then the Spaniards slipped in and used this treachery as an outright victory. And since, at Ligny in Barrois, where the Count de Brienne was in command, when the emperor in person besieged the place and Bertheville, the count's lieutenant, went out to parley, the town was taken while he was negotiating.

Who was battering Capua when his soldiers, slack in their guard during a parley, let the French into the place unawares.

## modern-en · ariosto · 6:7

["Victory is always praiseworthy, whether it is gained by fortune or by skill." — Ariosto, xv. 1.]

The Italian poet of the Orlando Furioso.

## modern-en · chrysippus · 6:8

But the philosopher Chrysippus held the opposite view, and I share it. He used to say that those who run a race ought to put all their force into what they are doing and run as fast as they can; but that it is by no means fair to lay a hand on a rival to slow him, or stick out a leg to trip him up. And more generous still was the answer of the great Alexander to Polypercon, who urged him to take advantage of the night's darkness to fall on Darius: "Not at all," he said; "it is not for such a man as I am to steal a victory. Malo me fortunae poeniteat, quam victoria pudeat."

The Stoic philosopher who held that a runner should use every force he has, but may not lay a hand on his adversary to stop him or set a leg before him to throw him down. Montaigne agrees.

## modern-en · henry-vii · 7:0

There is a saying that death discharges us of all our obligations. I know some who have taken it in another sense. Henry VII of England agreed with Don Philip, son of the emperor Maximilian — or, to give him a more honourable title, father of the Emperor Charles V — that Philip should hand over the Duke of Suffolk of the White Rose, Henry's enemy, who had fled into the Low Countries. Philip duly did so, but on condition that Henry would not attempt the duke's life. But when Henry came to die, he commanded his son in his last will to put the duke to death as soon as he himself was dead. And lately, in the tragedy the Duke of Alva put on for us at Brussels in the persons of the Counts Horn and Egmont, there were several striking moments. One in particular: Count Egmont (on whose word and faith Count Horn had come and surrendered himself to the Duke of Alva) earnestly asked to be allowed to mount the scaffold first, so that death might discharge him of the obligation he had given the other. In that case it seems to me that death did not in fact acquit the former of his promise, and that the latter was discharged from it without dying. We cannot be bound beyond what we are able to perform, since effect and performance are not really in our power: indeed the only thing we are masters of is the will, and on the will, by necessity, all the rules and the whole duty of mankind are founded and established. Count Egmont, therefore, regarding his soul and his will as still bound by his promise even though he had not the power to make it good, would no doubt have been absolved of his duty even if he had outlived the other. But the King of England, who deliberately and with full intent broke his faith, was no more to be excused for putting off the execution of his infidelity until after his death than the mason in Herodotus, who kept the secret of his master the King of Egypt's treasure inviolate during his lifetime but at his own death revealed it to his children.

Who got the Duke of Suffolk delivered into his hands on condition that he attempt nothing against the man’s life, and then commanded his son in his will to put him to death as soon as he himself was dead. Montaigne will not allow that death excused the broken faith.

But when Henry came to die, he commanded his son in his last will to put the duke to death as soon as he himself was dead. And lately, in the tragedy the Duke of Alva put on for us at Brussels in the persons of the Counts Horn and Egmont, there were several striking moments. One in particular: Count Egmont (on whose word and faith Count Horn had come and surrendered himself to the Duke of Alva) earnestly asked to be allowed to mount the scaffold first, so that death might discharge him of the obligation he had given the other. In that case it seems to me that death did not in fact acquit the former of his promise, and that the latter was discharged from it without dying. We cannot be bound beyond what we are able to perform, since effect and performance are not really in our power: indeed the only thing we are masters of is the will, and on the will, by necessity, all the rules and the whole duty of mankind are founded and established. Count Egmont, therefore, regarding his soul and his will as still bound by his promise even though he had not the power to make it good, would no doubt have been absolved of his duty even if he had outlived the other. But the King of England, who deliberately and with full intent broke his faith, was no more to be excused for putting off the execution of his infidelity until after his death than the mason in Herodotus, who kept the secret of his master the King of Egypt's treasure inviolate during his lifetime but at his own death revealed it to his children.

Who staged the executions of the Counts Horn and Egmont at Brussels.

## modern-en · count-egmont · 7:0

There is a saying that death discharges us of all our obligations. I know some who have taken it in another sense. Henry VII of England agreed with Don Philip, son of the emperor Maximilian — or, to give him a more honourable title, father of the Emperor Charles V — that Philip should hand over the Duke of Suffolk of the White Rose, Henry's enemy, who had fled into the Low Countries. Philip duly did so, but on condition that Henry would not attempt the duke's life. But when Henry came to die, he commanded his son in his last will to put the duke to death as soon as he himself was dead. And lately, in the tragedy the Duke of Alva put on for us at Brussels in the persons of the Counts Horn and Egmont, there were several striking moments. One in particular: Count Egmont (on whose word and faith Count Horn had come and surrendered himself to the Duke of Alva) earnestly asked to be allowed to mount the scaffold first, so that death might discharge him of the obligation he had given the other. In that case it seems to me that death did not in fact acquit the former of his promise, and that the latter was discharged from it without dying. We cannot be bound beyond what we are able to perform, since effect and performance are not really in our power: indeed the only thing we are masters of is the will, and on the will, by necessity, all the rules and the whole duty of mankind are founded and established. Count Egmont, therefore, regarding his soul and his will as still bound by his promise even though he had not the power to make it good, would no doubt have been absolved of his duty even if he had outlived the other. But the King of England, who deliberately and with full intent broke his faith, was no more to be excused for putting off the execution of his infidelity until after his death than the mason in Herodotus, who kept the secret of his master the King of Egypt's treasure inviolate during his lifetime but at his own death revealed it to his children.

On whose word and faith Count Horn had surrendered himself to the Duke of Alva, and who begged to mount the scaffold first so that death might discharge him of the obligation. Montaigne’s verdict is that death did not acquit him and that he was already discharged without dying, since no man can be bound beyond what he is able to perform.

## modern-en · darius-i · 9:2

Secondly, this way I remember the injuries I have received less. As the ancient said, I should need a register of injuries, or a prompter, like Darius, who, so as not to forget the offence he had received from the people of Athens, had a page repeat in his ear three times every time he sat down to dinner: "Sir, remember the Athenians." And then again, the places I revisit, and the books I read over again, still smile on me with a fresh novelty.

The Persian king who kept a prompter for his injuries: so often as he sat down to dinner, a page was to say three times in his ear, “Sir, remember the Athenians.”

## modern-en · francis-i · 9:8

King Francis I boasted that he had by this means tied up Francesco Taverna, the ambassador of Francesco Sforza, Duke of Milan — a man very famous in his day for his skill in talking. This gentleman had been sent to excuse his master to His Majesty in a very serious business, which was this: the king, in order to keep up some intelligence in Italy, out of which he had lately been driven, and particularly with the Duchy of Milan, had thought it convenient to keep a gentleman of his own on hand with the duke — an ambassador in effect, but in outward appearance a private person who was supposed to be residing there on his own affairs. The duke, being much more dependent on the emperor (especially while he was negotiating his marriage with the emperor's niece, daughter of the King of Denmark, who is now the dowager of Lorraine), could not show any open dealings or conferences with us without serious risk. For this commission, one Merveille, a Milanese gentleman and an equerry of the king, was thought very suitable, and was sent there with private credentials and instructions as ambassador, along with other letters of recommendation to the duke concerning his own private affairs, the better to mask and colour the business. He was so long at that court that the emperor at last got wind of his real employment there — which was the cause, we suppose, of what followed: under pretext of some murder, his trial was dispatched in two days, and his head was struck off in prison at night. Messire Francesco arrived in France prepared with a long counterfeit account of the affair (for the king had applied to all the princes of Christendom, as well as to the duke himself, to demand satisfaction) and was given his audience at the morning council. There, after he had laid out several plausible justifications of the fact in support of his case — that his master had never looked on Merveille as anything but a private gentleman and his own subject, who was there only on his own business, and had never lived under any other character; absolutely denying that he had ever heard he was a member of the king's household, or that His Majesty so much as knew him, so far was he from taking him for an ambassador — the king, pressing him in turn with several objections and demands and challenging him from every side, tripped him up at last by asking, then, why the execution had been carried out at night, and as it were by stealth. At which the poor confounded ambassador, the more elegantly to extricate himself, replied that the duke would have been very loath, out of respect for His Majesty, to have such an execution carried out by day. One can imagine how he was scolded when he got home, for having so grossly tripped in the presence of a prince of so delicate a nose as King Francis.

Francis I of France, a prince, Montaigne says, of so delicate a nostril that he caught Francesco Taverna out in a single question, and who was still keeping a secret agent in Milan when Merveille was beheaded there.

Asked by King Francis why then the thing had been done at night and as it were by stealth, he answered that the Duke would have been loth, out of respect to his Majesty, to have it done by day — and was, Montaigne guesses, well rated for it when he got home.

## modern-en · julius-ii · 9:9

Pope Julius II, having sent an ambassador to the King of England to stir him up against King Francis, had his ambassador heard. Before he would give an answer, the king urged the difficulties he would meet in setting on foot so great a preparation as would be needed to attack so powerful a king, and gave his reasons. The ambassador very unseasonably replied that he too had considered the same difficulties and had laid them before the pope. From this saying, so directly contrary to the matter in hand and the business he had come for (which was simply to incite the king to war), the King of England first drew the conclusion (which he later found to be true) that this ambassador, in his own heart, was on the French side. He notified the pope, the ambassador's estate was confiscated at his return, and he himself only narrowly escaped losing his head.

Who sent an ambassador to the King of England to set him against King Francis — and whose ambassador argued himself out of his own errand by agreeing that the war would be hard to mount.

## modern-en · la-brebis · 10:1

["All graces were never given to any one man." — A line from one of La Brebis's sonnets.]

The poet whose sonnet supplies the line that all graces were never yet given to any one man. The note gives no more than the name.

## modern-en · clement-vii · 10:3

Just as we teach young ladies the sports and exercises best fitted to show off the grace and beauty of those parts where their chief charm and perfection lie, so it should be with these two advantages of eloquence which the lawyers and preachers of our age principally lay claim to. If I were worthy to advise, the slow speaker, it seems to me, would be better suited to the pulpit and the other to the bar — for the preacher's office naturally allows him all the leisure he might want to prepare himself, and his career runs in an even, uninterrupted line, with no break or stop, whereas the pleader's business and interest compels him to enter the lists on every occasion, and the unexpected objections and replies of his opponent throw him out of his course and force him, on the instant, to think up new and extempore answers and defences. Yet at the meeting between Pope Clement and King Francis at Marseilles, it happened quite the contrary: Monsieur Poyet, a man who had spent his whole life at the bar and had the highest reputation for eloquence, was charged with making the address to the pope. Having so long meditated it beforehand that, they say, he brought it ready-made with him from Paris, on the very day it was to be delivered the pope, fearing that something might be said that would give offence to the other princes' ambassadors who were attending him there, sent word to the king of the line he thought best suited to the time and place — quite another thing, as it happened, from what Monsieur de Poyet had taken so much trouble over. So that the fine speech he had prepared was no use, and he had to come up with another on the spot, which, finding himself unable to do, Cardinal du Bellay had to step in and perform that office. The pleader's part is no doubt much harder than the preacher's, and yet, in my view, we see in France more passable lawyers than preachers. It seems that wit's nature is to be prompt and sudden in its operations, while judgment is more deliberate and slow. But he who stays totally silent for want of leisure to prepare himself to speak well, and he whom leisure does not help to better speech, are equally unfortunate.

Who at the interview with King Francis at Marseilles sent word of the argument he thought suitable, and so wasted the harangue Poyet had been preparing since Paris.

## modern-en · severus-cassius · 10:4

It is said of Severus Cassius that he spoke best extempore, that he owed more to fortune than to his own diligence; that it was an advantage to him to be interrupted in speaking, and that his opponents were afraid to provoke him, lest his anger should redouble his eloquence. I know from experience a temperament of nature so impatient of any tedious and elaborate preparation that, unless it goes frankly and gaily about its work, it cannot do anything to purpose. We say of certain compositions that they smell of the oil and the lamp, because of a certain rough harshness that laborious handling stamps on whatever it has been used on. Beyond this, the anxiety of doing well, and the kind of straining and striving of a mind too far stretched and overbent on its task, breaks and frustrates itself like water that, by the force of its own pressing violence and abundance, cannot find a ready way out through the neck of a bottle or a narrow sluice. In the kind of nature I am now speaking of, there is this too — that it would not be disordered and stimulated by such passions as the fury of Cassius (such a motion would be too violent and rough); it would not be jostled, but coaxed; it would be roused and warmed by unexpected, sudden, and accidental occasions. Left to itself, it flags and languishes; agitation alone gives it grace and vigour. I am always at my worst in my own possession, and when wholly at my own disposal: chance has more claim on whatever comes from me than I do; occasion, company, and even the very rising and falling of my own voice draw more out of my mind than I can find when I sound and apply it by myself. So the things I say are better than those I write, if either were worth choosing where neither is worth much. This also happens to me: that I do not find myself where I look for myself, and I hit on things more by chance than by any active search of my own judgment. I sometimes happen to write something that strikes me as neat and lively, though it will appear dull and heavy to someone else — but enough of these fine compliments; every man talks like this of himself, according to his talent. When I come to speak, however, I am already so lost that I do not know what I was about to say, and on these occasions a stranger has often spotted it before me. If I made an erasure every time this inconvenience overtook me, I should leave nothing standing; occasion, on some other day, will lay the thought as plain to me as the light, and make me wonder why I should have hesitated.

Who spoke best extempore and owed more to fortune than to diligence: it was an advantage to him to be interrupted, and his adversaries were afraid to nettle him for fear his anger should redouble his eloquence.

## modern-en · jesus-christ · 11:0

As to oracles, it is certain that a good while before the coming of Jesus Christ they had begun to lose their credit. Cicero, for instance, was at a loss to find the cause of their decay, and uses these words:

Whose coming Montaigne uses to date the decay of the oracles, and whose thirty-three years he sets beside Alexander’s as the measure of a life that was long enough.

## modern-en · pacuvius · 11:24

Pacuvius is wiser by far —

The early Latin tragedian, quoted through Cicero for the wisest word in the chapter: as for those who understand the language of birds, I had rather hear them than attend to them.

## modern-en · francesco-saluzzo · 11:14

— yet they carry far less authority now than they used to. Which makes all the more remarkable the case of Francesco, Marquis of Saluzzo, lieutenant to King Francis I in his ultramontane army, much favoured and respected at our court, and beholden to the king's bounty for the very marquisate itself, which had been forfeited by his brother. With no real provocation given him, and with his own affection set against any such disloyalty, he let himself be terrified, as the report ran, by the fine forecasts that were being put out everywhere in favour of the Emperor Charles V, and to our disadvantage — especially in Italy, where these foolish prophecies were so far believed that at Rome large sums of money were being wagered on the returns when the prognostics came true, so sure were they of our ruin. Having often bewailed to those of his closest acquaintance the disasters he saw bound to fall on the Crown of France and on the friends he had at that court, he revolted and went over to the other side — to his own misfortune, however, whatever stars were ruling at the time. But he carried himself in this affair like a man pulled by different passions: with towns and forces in his hands, the enemy's army under Antonio de Leyva close by him, and we suspecting nothing of his design, it had been in his power to do far more than he did. We lost no men through this faithlessness of his, nor any town except Fossano, and that only after a long siege and a brave defence.

Lieutenant to Francis I in the army beyond the mountains, holding the marquisate by the king’s gift, with no provocation to turn and his own affection against it — and frightened into revolt, it was said, by the prognostics that everyone was spreading in Charles V’s favour. Montaigne notes that with towns and troops in his hands he could have done far more harm than he did.

## modern-en · antonio-de-leyva · 11:14

— yet they carry far less authority now than they used to. Which makes all the more remarkable the case of Francesco, Marquis of Saluzzo, lieutenant to King Francis I in his ultramontane army, much favoured and respected at our court, and beholden to the king's bounty for the very marquisate itself, which had been forfeited by his brother. With no real provocation given him, and with his own affection set against any such disloyalty, he let himself be terrified, as the report ran, by the fine forecasts that were being put out everywhere in favour of the Emperor Charles V, and to our disadvantage — especially in Italy, where these foolish prophecies were so far believed that at Rome large sums of money were being wagered on the returns when the prognostics came true, so sure were they of our ruin. Having often bewailed to those of his closest acquaintance the disasters he saw bound to fall on the Crown of France and on the friends he had at that court, he revolted and went over to the other side — to his own misfortune, however, whatever stars were ruling at the time. But he carried himself in this affair like a man pulled by different passions: with towns and forces in his hands, the enemy's army under Antonio de Leyva close by him, and we suspecting nothing of his design, it had been in his power to do far more than he did. We lost no men through this faithlessness of his, nor any town except Fossano, and that only after a long siege and a brave defence.

Whose army lay close by Saluzzo when he revolted.

## modern-en · tages · 11:27

The famous art of divination among the Tuscans began like this: a labourer, striking deep into the earth with his ploughshare, saw the demi-god Tages rise up, with an infant's face but armed with mature and senile wisdom. On the rumour of this, all the people came running to see the sight, and his words and science — containing the principles and means of attaining this art — were recorded and kept for many ages. A birth in keeping with its later course. For my part, I would sooner regulate my affairs by the throw of a die than by such idle and empty dreams. And indeed, in every republic, a good share of the government has always been left to chance. Plato, in the civil polity he models on his own fancy, leaves it to decide several matters of great importance, and is willing, among other things, that marriages should be assigned by lot; he attaches such weight to this accidental choice as to require that the children born in such weddings be brought up in the country, and that those born in any other wedding be cast out as illegitimate and base. He adds, however, that if any of those exiles, as they grow, give signs of merit, they may be recalled; and that some of those who were kept may, in the same way, be exiled if they show little promise as they grow up.

The demigod who rose out of a furrow with an infant’s face and an old man’s wisdom, and dictated the principles of Tuscan divination to the people who came running to look at him. Montaigne says the birth suits the art.

## modern-en · diogenes-the-atheist · 11:31

I think no better of them for some such accidental hit. There would be more certainty about it if there were a rule, and a truth, of always lying. Besides, no one keeps a record of their flimflams and false prognostics, since they are infinite and commonplace; but if they chance upon a single truth, that makes a mighty noise as being rare, incredible, and prodigious. So Diogenes, nicknamed the Atheist, answered the man in Samothrace who showed him in the temple the various offerings and paintings of those who had escaped shipwreck and said to him: "You who think the gods take no care of human things, what do you say to so many people preserved from death by their particular favour?" "Why," Diogenes answered, "the pictures aren't here of those who were drowned — who are by far the greater number."

Shown, in the temple at Samothrace, the offerings and painted stories of men saved from shipwreck by the gods’ especial favour, he answered that the pictures of those who were cast away are not here, and they are by much the greater number.

## modern-en · xenophanes-colophon · 11:32

Cicero observes that of all the philosophers who have acknowledged a deity, only Xenophanes of Colophon has tried to root out every kind of divination — which makes it less of a wonder if we have now and then seen some of our princes, sometimes to their own cost, lean too heavily on these vanities. I would have given anything to see with my own eyes those two great marvels: the book of Joachim, the Calabrian abbot, which foretold all the future popes, with their names and characters; and that of the Emperor Leo, which prophesied all the emperors and patriarchs of Greece. This much I have witnessed myself: that in times of public confusion, men astonished at their fortune abandon their own reason and superstitiously seek out in the stars the ancient causes and warnings of their present troubles. In my time they have been so strangely successful in this that it has made me believe — this being an amusement for sharp and volatile minds — that those skilled at this knack of unfolding and untying riddles are capable of finding in any kind of writing whatever they wish. But above all, the greatest room for play is given them by the obscure, ambiguous, and fantastical gibberish of prophetic jargon, where the authors deliver nothing of clear sense but shroud everything in riddles, so that posterity may interpret and apply them according to its own fancy.

The only philosopher who acknowledged a deity and still tried to root out divination altogether — as Cicero observes, and Montaigne with him.

## modern-en · laches · 12:1

Several very warlike nations have used a retreating and flying way of fighting as a singular advantage, and by doing so have made their backs more dangerous to their enemies than their faces. The Turks still keep something of this in their practice of arms. Socrates, in Plato, laughs at Laches, who had defined fortitude as standing firm in the ranks against the enemy. "What!" he says, "would it then be cowardice to overcome them by giving ground?" — urging at the same time the authority of Homer, who praises in Aeneas the science of flight. And when Laches, thinking better of it, admits the practice as it applies to the Scythians and, in general, to all cavalry, Socrates attacks him again with the example of the Lacedaemonian foot — a nation of all others the most stubborn in holding their ground — who, at the battle of Plataea, finding they could not break into the Persian phalanx, decided to scatter and retreat, so that the enemy, supposing they fled, should break up and disunite that great mass of men in pursuit; and by this stratagem they won the victory.

Who defined fortitude in Plato as standing firm in the ranks, and was laughed out of it by Socrates: would it be cowardice, then, to beat the enemy by giving ground?

## modern-en · idanthyrses · 12:2

As for the Scythians, it is said that when Darius marched out to subdue them, he sent a herald to reproach their king with always retiring before him and avoiding battle. Idanthyrses — that was the king's name — answered that it was not for fear of him or any man living that he did so, but that it was the way of marching practised by his nation, who had neither tilled fields, nor cities, nor houses to defend, nor anything they need fear the enemy might profit by; but, if he had such a stomach to fight, let him just come to view their ancient burial places, and there he should have his fill.

The Scythian king who told Darius that he did not retire out of fear but because his nation had neither fields nor cities to lose, and that if Darius wanted a battle he had only to come and look at their ancient places of sepulture.

## modern-en · guast · 12:3

Nevertheless, against cannon-fire, when a body of men is drawn up in front of a train of artillery — as the demands of war often require — it is unbecoming to leave one's post to avoid the danger, since because of its violence and swiftness we reckon it inevitable; and many a man, by ducking, stepping aside, and other such motions of fear, has been thoroughly laughed at by his companions. And yet, in the campaign the Emperor Charles V made into Provence against us, the Marquis del Guasto, going to reconnoitre the city of Arles and stepping out from behind the cover of a windmill under which he had made his approach, was spotted by the Seigneur de Bonneval and the Seneschal of Agenais, who were walking on the theatre aux arenes. They pointed him out to the Sieur de Villiers, commissary of the artillery, who pointed a culverin so admirably well and aimed it so exactly that, had the marquis not slipped aside when he saw the match put to it, it was reckoned certain the shot would have taken him full in the body. And in the same way, some years earlier, Lorenzo de' Medici, Duke of Urbino and father of the queen-mother, laying siege to Mondolfo, a place in the territories of the Vicariate in Italy, saw the gunner put the match to a piece pointed straight at him. It was well for him that he ducked, for otherwise the shot, which only grazed the top of his head, would no doubt have hit him full in the chest. To tell the truth, I do not think these evasions are performed out of any judgment, for how can any man living judge of high or low aim on so sudden an occasion? It is much easier to believe that fortune favoured their apprehension, and that it might just as well, on another occasion, lead them to face the danger as to seek to avoid it. For my own part, I confess I cannot help starting when the rattle of a harquebus thunders in my ears suddenly, and in a place where I had not expected it — something I have observed in others too, braver fellows than I.

Who went to reconnoitre Arles from behind a windmill and was spotted; the culverin was so exactly levelled at him that only his seeing the fire given and slipping aside saved him.

## modern-en · virgil · 12:6

["Though tears fall, the mind remains unmoved." — Virgil, Aeneid, iv. 449.]

The Latin poet of the Aeneid, the Georgics and the Eclogues. The citations run under the poem’s name as often as his own, spelled AEneid in the older edition and Aeneid in the modern.

## modern-en · margaret-of-navarre · 13:0

There is no subject so trivial that it does not deserve a place in this rhapsody. By our common rule of civility, it would be a marked insult to an equal, and much more to a superior, to fail to be at home when he has told you he will come to visit. Queen Margaret of Navarre adds, further, that it would be a rudeness in a gentleman to go out, as we so often do, to meet a man who is coming to see him, however high his condition may be; that it is more respectful and more civil to stay at home to receive him, if only to avoid missing him on the road, and that it is enough to receive him at the door and to attend on him there. For my part, anxious as I am to reduce the ceremonies of my house, I very often forget both these vain offices. If by chance someone is offended at this, I cannot help it; better to offend him once than to offend myself every day, for it would be a perpetual slavery. To what end do we avoid the servile attendance of courts, if we bring the same trouble back into our own private houses? It is also a common rule at all assemblies that the lesser sort should be on the place first, since it is more fitting to the better sort to make others wait and expect them.

Who held that a gentleman is rude to go out and meet a visitor, and that it is more civil to stay at home and receive him at the door.

## modern-en · montmorency · 14:1

The Constable Monsieur de Montmorency, having at the siege of Pavia been ordered to cross the Ticino and take up his quarters in the Faubourg St Antonio, was held up by a tower at the end of the bridge so stubborn as to endure a battery; for which he hanged every man he found inside. And again, accompanying the Dauphin in his expedition over the Alps, and taking the castle of Villano by assault, where all inside had been put to the sword by the fury of the soldiers, the governor and his ensign alone excepted, he had these two trussed up too for the same reason. So also did Captain Martin du Bellay, then governor of Turin, with the governor of San Buono, in that same country, all of whose people had been cut to pieces at the taking of the place.

Who at the siege of Pavia hanged every man in the tower that had held up his crossing of the Ticino, and later trussed up the governor and ensign of the Castle of Villano for the same reason — the custom Montaigne is explaining, that a place not tenable by the rules of war may be defended only at the defenders’ own risk.

## modern-en · vervins · 15:0

I once heard tell of a prince, and a great captain, who, when at his table the proceedings against Monsieur de Vervins were related to him — Vervins had been sentenced to death for surrendering Boulogne to the English — openly maintained that a soldier cannot justly be put to death for lack of courage. And, in truth, there is reason to make a great distinction between faults that merely proceed from weakness and those that are clearly the effects of treachery and malice. In the latter, we act against the rules of reason that nature has imprinted in us; whereas, in the former, it seems we might appeal to the same nature, which left us in such a state of imperfection and weakness of courage, in our own defence. So that many have thought we ought to be answerable only for what we commit against our conscience; and it is partly on this rule that those base their opinion who disapprove of capital or bloody punishments for heretics and unbelievers, and those also who hold that a judge is not accountable for having failed in his administration through mere ignorance.

Sentenced to death for surrendering Boulogne to the English — the case that prompted a great captain to maintain, at table, that no soldier can justly be put to death for want of courage.

## modern-en · charondas · 15:1

But as to cowardice, it is certain that the most usual way of punishing it is by public disgrace; and the practice is supposed to have been first brought into use by the legislator Charondas. Before his time the laws of Greece punished those who fled in battle with death; he ordained that they should only, for three days, be exposed in the public square in women's clothes, hoping still to get some service from them by waking up their courage through this open shame:

The legislator who, Montaigne says, brought in ignominy instead of death for cowardice: before him Greek law killed the man who fled a battle, and he ordained instead that he be exposed three days in public in woman’s clothes, in hope of getting some service out of him afterwards.

## modern-en · tertullian · 15:3

["Rather to bring the blood into a man's cheek than to let it out of his body." — Tertullian, Apologetics.]

Quoted from the Apologetics for the maxim behind Charondas’s law: rather bring the blood into a man’s cheek than let it out of his body.

## modern-en · ammianus-marcellinus · 15:4

It also appears that the Roman laws once punished those with death who had run away; for Ammianus Marcellinus says that the Emperor Julian commanded ten of his soldiers, who had turned their backs in an encounter against the Parthians, to be first degraded, and afterwards put to death, in accordance, he says, with the ancient laws. And yet elsewhere, for the same kind of offence, he only condemned others to remain among the prisoners under the baggage ensign. The harsh punishment that the people of Rome inflicted on those who fled from the battle of Cannae, and on those who ran away with Cnaeus Fulvius at his defeat, did not extend to death. And yet I think there is a danger that disgrace may make such offenders desperate, and turn them not merely into half-hearted friends but into actual enemies.

The late Roman historian, cited for the Emperor Julian’s degrading and then executing ten soldiers who turned their backs on the Parthians.

## modern-en · julian · 15:4

And yet elsewhere, for the same kind of offence, he only condemned others to remain among the prisoners under the baggage ensign. And yet I think there is a danger that disgrace may make such offenders desperate, and turn them not merely into half-hearted friends but into actual enemies.

And yet I think there is a danger that disgrace may make such offenders desperate, and turn them not merely into half-hearted friends but into actual enemies.

Whose men ran away at his defeat and were punished, like those who fled at Cannae, with something short of death. The older edition prints him Aeneius Fulvius.

## modern-en · frauget · 15:5

Of recent memory, the Seigneur de Franget, lieutenant to the Maréchal de Châtillon's company, having by the Maréchal de Chabannes been put in charge of Fuenterrabia in place of Monsieur de Lude, and having surrendered it to the Spaniards, was for this condemned to be stripped of all nobility, and both he and his descendants declared base, taxable, and forever incapable of bearing arms — a severe sentence afterwards duly executed at Lyon. And since, all the gentlemen who were in Guise when the Count of Nassau entered it underwent the same punishment, as several others have done since for the like offence. Nevertheless, in a case of such manifest ignorance or cowardice as exceeds all ordinary example, it is only reasonable to take it as sufficient proof of treachery and malice, and to punish accordingly.

Lieutenant to the Mareschal de Chatillon’s company and put in command of Fuentarabia, who surrendered it to the Spaniard and was degraded from all nobility — himself and his posterity declared ignoble, taxable and for ever incapable of bearing arms, a sentence executed at Lyons.

## modern-en · lude · 15:5

Of recent memory, the Seigneur de Franget, lieutenant to the Maréchal de Châtillon's company, having by the Maréchal de Chabannes been put in charge of Fuenterrabia in place of Monsieur de Lude, and having surrendered it to the Spaniards, was for this condemned to be stripped of all nobility, and both he and his descendants declared base, taxable, and forever incapable of bearing arms — a severe sentence afterwards duly executed at Lyon. And since, all the gentlemen who were in Guise when the Count of Nassau entered it underwent the same punishment, as several others have done since for the like offence. Nevertheless, in a case of such manifest ignorance or cowardice as exceeds all ordinary example, it is only reasonable to take it as sufficient proof of treachery and malice, and to punish accordingly.

Whom Frauget replaced at Fuentarabia.

## modern-en · nassau-guise · 15:5

Who entered Guise, after which all the gentlemen who had been in the town were degraded like Frauget. The Essays do not say whether he is the Count of Nassau who besieged Mousson.

## modern-en · propertius · 16:2

["Let the sailor be content to talk of the winds; the cowherd of his oxen; the soldier of his wounds; the shepherd of his flocks." — An Italian rendering of Propertius, ii. 1, 43.]

The Latin elegiac poet. The older edition prints him Propertious in one citation and Propertius in another.

## modern-en · archidamus · 16:3

For it often happens, on the contrary, that everyone would rather hold forth on someone else's province than on his own, thinking it so much extra reputation acquired — witness the jeer Archidamus put upon Periander, that "he had given up the glory of being an excellent physician for the reputation of a very bad poet." And just observe how lavish and full Caesar is when he wants to make us understand his inventions for building bridges and contriving engines of war, and how succinct and reserved by comparison when he speaks of the offices of his profession, his own valour, and military conduct. His deeds prove him a great captain well enough, and he knew this; but he also wanted to be thought an excellent engineer — a quality somewhat outside the field, and not one we should look for in him. The elder Dionysius was a very great captain, as his fortune required; but he took a great deal of trouble to gain a private reputation as a poet, and yet he was never cut out for one. A man of the legal profession, brought not long ago to see a study furnished with all sorts of books, on his own subject and on every other, took no interest at all in any of them, but fell rather rudely and magisterially to comment on a barricade set across the spiral staircase in front of the door of the study — a thing a hundred captains and common soldiers see every day without noticing or finding fault with.

The Spartan king who told Periander that he had given up the glory of being an excellent physician to gain the repute of a very bad poet.

A man of the legal profession, brought not long ago to see a study furnished with all sorts of books, on his own subject and on every other, took no interest at all in any of them, but fell rather rudely and magisterially to comment on a barricade set across the spiral staircase in front of the door of the study — a thing a hundred captains and common soldiers see every day without noticing or finding fault with.

Julius Caesar, whose Commentaries Montaigne reads as a soldier’s book with a vanity in it: large and ample about his bridges and engines, succinct and reserved about his own valour and generalship, because the generalship was not in doubt and the engineering was.

## modern-en · langey · 16:8

Which is why (something I would have lightly passed over in another writer) I lingered over and carefully considered one passage in the history written by Monsieur de Langey, a man of very great judgment in matters of that kind. After giving an account of the fine oration Charles V had made in the Consistory at Rome, in the presence of the Bishop of Macon and Monsieur du Velly, our ambassadors there, in which he had mixed in several injurious expressions to the dishonour of our nation — and, among the rest, "that if his captains and soldiers were not men of another stamp of fidelity, resolution, and skill in arms than those of the king, he would put a rope round his neck and immediately go to him to beg for mercy" (and the emperor seems really to have held this, or only a very slightly better, opinion of our military men, for he afterwards, twice or three times in his life, said the very same thing); as also, that he challenged the king to fight him in his shirt, with rapier and dagger, in a boat. The said Sieur de Langey, going on with his history, adds that the said ambassadors, sending a dispatch to the king on these matters, concealed the greater part, and in particular the last two passages. At which I could not but wonder, that it should be in an ambassador's power to suppress anything he ought to convey to his master, especially something of such importance as this, coming from the mouth of such a person and spoken in so great an assembly. And I should rather have thought it the servant's duty faithfully to lay the whole matter before him as it passed, so that the liberty of selecting, arranging, judging, and concluding should remain with him. For to conceal or disguise the truth out of fear that he may take it otherwise than he ought, and out of fear it may prompt him to some extreme resolution — and in the meantime to leave him in the dark about his own affairs — seems to me to belong rather to the man who is to give the law than to the one who is only to receive it; to the one in supreme command, and not to one who ought to regard himself as inferior, not only in authority but also in prudence and good counsel. I, for my part, would not be served so even in my own small affairs.

The historian of the French wars whose account of Charles V’s speech in the Consistory at Rome Montaigne dwells on, because the ambassadors who heard it sent home a despatch with the worst of it left out.

## modern-en · publius-crassus · 16:9

We so willingly slip the collar of command on any pretext, and are so ready to usurp dominion, every one of us so naturally aspires to liberty and power, that no advantage a superior gets from the wit or valour of those he employs ought to be as dear to him as a straightforward and sincere obedience. To obey by reason of one's own understanding rather than out of subjection is to corrupt the office of command. So it was that Publius Crassus, the man whom the Romans reckoned five times happy, when he was consul in Asia sent for a Greek engineer to bring him the larger of two ships' masts he had noticed at Athens, for use in some battery engine he meant to make. The engineer, presuming on his own skill and sufficiency in such matters, thought fit to do otherwise than directed, and brought the smaller one, which, by the rules of his art, was really more proper for the use in hand. But Crassus, though he listened to his reasons very patiently, would not, however well-grounded they were, take them as current pay, but had him soundly whipped for his pains, valuing the interest of discipline far more than the interest of the work in hand.

Whom the Romans reputed five times happy. Consul in Asia, he sent a Greek engineer for the greater of two ships’ masts, and had the man whipped for bringing the lesser — which was, by the rules of art, the fitter of the two. Montaigne sets discipline against the work in hand and then, at the end of the chapter, wonders whether Crassus had not invited the man’s judgment after all.

## modern-en · bourbon · 17:2

I am not naturalist enough (as they call it) to make out by what secret springs fear has its motion in us; but, that aside, it is a strange passion, and one that the physicians say there is no other that more quickly dethrones our judgment from its proper seat. So true is this that I myself have seen many people become frantic with fear, and even in those of the steadiest temper it is certain that during the fit it produces a terrible bewilderment and confusion. I leave aside the common run of people, to whom it represents one moment their great-grandfathers risen from their graves in their shrouds, and another moment werewolves, nightmares, and chimeras. But even among soldiers — a sort of men over whom fear, of all others, ought to have the least power — how often has it turned flocks of sheep into armed squadrons, reeds and bulrushes into pikes and lances, friends into enemies, and the white cross of France into the red cross of Spain! When Monsieur de Bourbon took Rome, an ensign on guard at Borgo San Pietro was seized with such fright at the first alarm that he threw himself out at a breach with his colours on his shoulder and ran straight toward the enemy, thinking he had retreated towards the inner defences of the city; and only with great trouble, when he saw Monsieur de Bourbon's men, who took it for a sally upon them, drawing up to receive him, did he at last come to himself and realise his mistake. Then facing about, he retreated at top speed back through the same breach by which he had come out, but only after he had first blindly advanced more than three hundred paces into the open field. It did not turn out so well with Captain Giulio's ensign at the time when St Paul was taken from us by the Count of Bures and Monsieur de Reu, for he, so amazed with fear that he flung himself, colours and all, out of a port-hole, was immediately cut to pieces by the enemy. And in the same siege there was a very memorable fear that so seized, contracted, and froze up the heart of a gentleman that he sank down, stone-dead, in the breach, without any manner of wound or hurt at all. The like madness sometimes pushes on a whole multitude: for in one of the encounters Germanicus had with the Germans, two great parties were so seized with fear that they ran two opposite ways, the one to the very place from which the other had fled. Sometimes fear adds wings to the heels, as in the first two cases; sometimes it nails them to the ground and fetters them from moving, as we read of the Emperor Theophilus, who in a battle he lost against the Agarenes was so astonished and stupefied that he had no power to fly —

Who took Rome — the sack in which an ensign on guard at Borgo San Pietro ran three hundred paces the wrong way in a fright, colours on his shoulder, and had to run back through the same breach.

Who lost a battle to the Agarenes and was so stupefied that he had no power to fly, until Manuel shook him out of it with an offer to kill him.

## modern-en · manuel · 17:5

— until Manuel, one of the principal commanders of his army, having jogged and shaken him to bring him out of his trance, said to him: "Sir, if you will not follow me, I will kill you, for it is better that you should lose your life than, by being taken, lose your empire." But fear shows its utmost power when it drives us into a valiant despair, having first stripped us of all sense of duty and honour. In the first pitched battle the Romans lost against Hannibal, under the consul Sempronius, a body of ten thousand foot that had taken fright, seeing no other escape for their cowardice, threw themselves headlong on the great battalion of the enemy, and with marvellous force and fury charged through and through it, routing it with a very great slaughter of the Carthaginians — buying themselves an inglorious flight at the same price they might have gained a glorious victory.

One of Theophilus’s principal commanders, who told his stupefied emperor that it was better to lose his life than, by being taken, to lose his empire.

## modern-en · quintus-curtius · 17:4

["So much does fear dread even the means of safety." — Quintus Curtius, ii. 11.]

The historian of Alexander, quoted for the line that fear dreads even the means of safety. The older edition abbreviates him Quint. Curt.

But fear shows its utmost power when it drives us into a valiant despair, having first stripped us of all sense of duty and honour. In the first pitched battle the Romans lost against Hannibal, under the consul Sempronius, a body of ten thousand foot that had taken fright, seeing no other escape for their cowardice, threw themselves headlong on the great battalion of the enemy, and with marvellous force and fury charged through and through it, routing it with a very great slaughter of the Carthaginians — buying themselves an inglorious flight at the same price they might have gained a glorious victory.

The Carthaginian general, in the first pitched battle against whom ten thousand Roman foot took fright, saw no other escape for their cowardice, and charged straight through the enemy’s great battalion — buying an ignominious flight at the price a glorious victory would have cost.

## modern-en · sempronius · 17:5

— until Manuel, one of the principal commanders of his army, having jogged and shaken him to bring him out of his trance, said to him: "Sir, if you will not follow me, I will kill you, for it is better that you should lose your life than, by being taken, lose your empire." But fear shows its utmost power when it drives us into a valiant despair, having first stripped us of all sense of duty and honour. In the first pitched battle the Romans lost against Hannibal, under the consul Sempronius, a body of ten thousand foot that had taken fright, seeing no other escape for their cowardice, threw themselves headlong on the great battalion of the enemy, and with marvellous force and fury charged through and through it, routing it with a very great slaughter of the Carthaginians — buying themselves an inglorious flight at the same price they might have gained a glorious victory.

## modern-en · croesus · 18:3

Even children know the story of King Croesus on this point. Taken prisoner by Cyrus and condemned by him to die, on his way to execution he cried out, "O Solon, Solon!" When this was reported to Cyrus, who sent to ask what it meant, Croesus explained that he was now finding, to his cost, how true the lesson Solon had once given him had been — namely, "that, however fortune may smile upon them, men can never be called happy until they have been seen to pass over the last day of their lives," because of the uncertainty and mutability of human things, which on the smallest and most trivial occasions are liable to be entirely changed into the contrary condition. And so it was that Agesilaus answered someone saying what a happy young man the King of Persia was, to come so young to so mighty a kingdom: "Yes," he said, "but neither was Priam unhappy at his years." In a short time, kings of Macedon, successors of that great Alexander, became carpenters and scribes at Rome; a tyrant of Sicily a schoolmaster at Corinth; a conqueror of half the world and general of so many armies, a wretched suppliant to the rascally officers of a king of Egypt — so much did the prolongation of five or six months of life cost the great Pompey. And in our fathers' days, Ludovico Sforza, the tenth Duke of Milan, under whom all Italy had so long bowed, was seen to die a wretched prisoner at Loches, but not until he had lived ten years in captivity, which was the worst part of his fortune. The fairest of all queens, widow to the greatest king in Europe — did she not come to die at the hand of an executioner? Unworthy and barbarous cruelty. And a thousand other examples there are of the same kind. It seems that, just as storms and tempests have a malice against the proud and overtowering heights of our lofty buildings, there are also spirits above that are envious of the greatnesses here below:

The King of Lydia, taken by Cyrus and led out to execution crying “O Solon, Solon!” — because he had found what Solon told him true to his cost: that no man can be called happy until he has been seen to pass over the last day of his life.

## modern-en · laberius · 18:6

And it would seem, too, that Fortune sometimes lies in wait to ambush the last hour of our lives, to show the power she has, in a moment, to overthrow what she had been so many years in building, and to make us cry out with Laberius:

## modern-en · macrobius · 18:8

["I have lived by this one day longer than I ought to have lived." — Macrobius, ii. 7.]

The late Latin compiler, cited for Laberius’s line.

## modern-en · metellus-scipio · 18:12

This is why, at this last moment, all the other actions of our life are to be tested and sifted. It is the master-day; it is the day that is judge of all the rest. "It is the day," says one of the ancients, "that must be judge of all my preceding years." To death I refer the assaying of the fruit of all my studies; then we shall see whether my discourses came only from my mouth, or from my heart. I have seen many men by their dying give a good or a bad name to their whole life. Scipio, Pompey's father-in-law, in dying well, removed the ill opinion everyone had until then conceived of him. Epaminondas, asked which of three he most esteemed, Chabrias, Iphicrates, or himself, said: "You must first see us die before that question can be answered." And indeed it would be a great wrong to weigh him without the honour and grandeur of his end.

Pompey’s father-in-law, who by the manner of his dying removed the ill opinion everyone had held of him until then.
