## modern-en · xenophilus · 19:5

Now, of all the benefits virtue confers on us, contempt of death is one of the greatest, because it is what fits human life with a soft and easy tranquillity, and gives us a pure and pleasant taste of living, without which all other pleasures would be extinguished. This is why all the rules centre and converge on this one article. And although they all likewise teach us, by common accord, to despise pain, poverty, and the other accidents human life is subject to, it is not with the same urgency — partly because those accidents are not so inevitable (the greater part of mankind pass their whole lives without ever knowing what poverty is, and some without sorrow or sickness, like Xenophilus the musician, who lived a hundred and six years in perfect and continual health); and partly because, at the worst, death can whenever we please cut short and put an end to all other inconveniences. But death itself is inevitable:

The musician who lived a hundred and six years in perfect and continual health — Montaigne’s example that poverty and sickness can be escaped, and death cannot.

## modern-en · tantalus · 19:10

["Ever, like Tantalus' stone, it hangs over us." — Cicero, De Finib., i. 18.]

Over whom the stone hangs for ever, in Cicero’s figure for death.

## modern-en · claudianus · 19:16

["He considers the route, counts the days of the journey, measures his life by the length of the road, and torments himself by thinking of the blow to come." — Claudianus, in Ruf., ii. 137.]

The late Latin poet, quoted for the condemned man who measures his life by the length of the road to the place of execution.

## modern-en · methuselah · 19:21

The Romans, finding that this poor syllable death sounded so harshly to their ears and seemed so ominous, found a way to soften and spin it out by a circumlocution. Instead of saying "So-and-so is dead," they said "So-and-so has lived," or "So-and-so has ceased to live" — for, provided there was any mention of life in the phrase, even past life, it carried some sound of consolation. From them we borrowed our expression, "The late Monsieur So-and-so." Perhaps, as the saying goes, the words we have lived are worth our money. I was born between eleven and twelve o'clock in the forenoon on the last day of February 1533, by our reckoning, which begins the year on the 1st of January; and it is now just fifteen days since I turned thirty-nine years old, complete. I expect to live, at least, as many more. In the meantime, it would be folly to trouble myself with the thought of a thing so far off. But what? Young and old die on the same terms; no one leaves life otherwise than as if he had just entered it. And no man is so old and decrepit that, having heard of Methuselah, he does not think he has another twenty good years to come. Fool that you are! Who has guaranteed you the term of your life? You depend on physicians' tales: rather consult facts and experience. By the common course of things, you have for some time been living by extraordinary favour; you have already outlived the ordinary span. To prove it, count up your acquaintances and see how many more have died before reaching your age than have made it; and of those who ennobled their lives by their fame, just keep a tally, and I will lay you a wager you will find more who died before than after thirty-five. It is both reasonable and pious to take Jesus Christ Himself for an example: He ended His life at thirty-three. The greatest man who was no more than a man, Alexander, died also at the same age. How many ways death has to surprise us!

Whose years are the reason, Montaigne says, that no man is so old and decrepit that he does not think he has twenty good ones left.

## modern-en · aeschylus · 19:24

To say nothing of fevers and pleurisies, who would ever have imagined that a Duke of Brittany would be crushed to death in a crowd, as that duke was at the entry of Pope Clement, my neighbour, into Lyons? Have you not seen one of our kings killed at a tilting match, and one of his ancestors die from being jostled by a hog? Aeschylus, warned of being killed by the fall of a house, took every possible precaution — and was knocked on the head by a tortoise dropped out of an eagle's talons in the air. Another man was choked by a grape-seed; an emperor was killed by the scratch of a comb while combing his hair. Aemilius Lepidus died by stumbling at his own threshold, and Aufidius by knocking against the door as he entered the council chamber. And between the very thighs of women: Cornelius Gallus the praetor; Tigillinus, captain of the watch at Rome; Ludovico, son of Guido di Gonzaga, Marquis of Mantua; and (a worse example) Speusippus, a Platonic philosopher, and one of our Popes. Poor Judge Bebius granted a postponement in a case for eight days; but in the meantime he himself was condemned by death, and his own stay of life expired. While Caius Julius the physician was anointing the eyes of a patient, death closed his own. And if I may bring in an example of my own blood: a brother of mine, Captain St. Martin, a young man of twenty-three who had already given sufficient proof of his courage, was playing a match at tennis when he received a blow from a ball a little above his right ear. As it left no sign of wound or bruise, he took no notice and did not even sit down to rest; nevertheless he died within five or six hours of an apoplexy caused by that blow.

Who took care to avoid the house that was threatening to fall on him, and was killed by a tortoise dropped out of an eagle’s talons.

Poor Judge Bebius granted a postponement in a case for eight days; but in the meantime he himself was condemned by death, and his own stay of life expired. While Caius Julius the physician was anointing the eyes of a patient, death closed his own. And if I may bring in an example of my own blood: a brother of mine, Captain St. Martin, a young man of twenty-three who had already given sufficient proof of his courage, was playing a match at tennis when he received a blow from a ball a little above his right ear. As it left no sign of wound or bruise, he took no notice and did not even sit down to rest; nevertheless he died within five or six hours of an apoplexy caused by that blow.

The physician whose own eyes death closed while he was anointing a patient’s.

## modern-en · captain-st-martin · 19:24

To say nothing of fevers and pleurisies, who would ever have imagined that a Duke of Brittany would be crushed to death in a crowd, as that duke was at the entry of Pope Clement, my neighbour, into Lyons? Have you not seen one of our kings killed at a tilting match, and one of his ancestors die from being jostled by a hog? Aeschylus, warned of being killed by the fall of a house, took every possible precaution — and was knocked on the head by a tortoise dropped out of an eagle's talons in the air. Another man was choked by a grape-seed; an emperor was killed by the scratch of a comb while combing his hair. Aemilius Lepidus died by stumbling at his own threshold, and Aufidius by knocking against the door as he entered the council chamber. As it left no sign of wound or bruise, he took no notice and did not even sit down to rest; nevertheless he died within five or six hours of an apoplexy caused by that blow.

Montaigne’s brother, twenty-three years old and already proved in the field, who took a tennis ball a little above the right ear, felt no wound, did not even sit down — and died of an apoplexy five or six hours later.

## modern-en · paulus-aemilius · 19:37

Where death waits for us is uncertain; let us look for him everywhere. The premeditation of death is the premeditation of liberty. He who has learned to die has unlearned to be a slave. There is nothing evil in life for him who rightly understands that the loss of life is no evil; to know how to die delivers us from all subjection and constraint. Paulus Aemilius answered the man whom the wretched King of Macedon, his prisoner, had sent to beg him not to lead him in his triumph: "Let him make that request to himself."

The Roman who conquered Macedon, and who answered the prisoner king’s request not to be led in the triumph with: let him make that request to himself.

## modern-en · lycurgus · 19:63

We must disengage ourselves from these common and hurtful humours. It was for this reason that men first placed cemeteries adjoining the churches, and in the most frequented parts of the city — to accustom, says Lycurgus, the common people, women, and children, so they should not be startled at the sight of a corpse; and that the continual sight of bones, graves, and funeral rites should keep us mindful of our frail condition:

The Spartan lawgiver, cited for putting the burying-places among the churches and the busiest parts of the city, so that the sight of bones and funerals should keep people in mind of their frail condition.

## modern-en · dicaearchus · 19:66

And as the Egyptians, after their feasts, used to present the company with a great image of death, while one cried out to them, "Drink and be merry, for such you shall be when you are dead," so it is my custom to have death not only in my imagination but continually in my mouth. There is nothing I am so curious to inform myself of as the manner of men's deaths — their words, their looks, their bearing; nor any passages in history I am so intent upon. It is plain enough, by my crowding in examples of this kind, that I have a particular fancy for the subject. If I were a writer of books, I would compile a register, with commentary, of the various deaths of men: he who would teach men to die would at the same time teach them to live. Dicaearchus made one and gave it that title, but designed it for a different and less profitable end.

Who compiled a register of the deaths of men — the book Montaigne says he would write himself, except that Dicaearchus meant it for a less profitable end. The older edition prints him Dicarchus.

["Alas, what a small portion of life remains to old men!" — Maximian, or Pseudo-Gallus, i. 16.]

The late Latin elegist, quoted for the line: alas, to old men what portion of life remains.

The Latin poet of the Astronomica, quoted twice in Nature’s speech: as we are born we die, and your grandsires saw no other thing than you.

## modern-en · thales · 19:122

"To what purpose should you try to draw back, if there is no way to evade it? You have seen plenty of examples of those who were well content to die, as thereby delivered from heavy miseries; but have you ever found any who were dissatisfied with dying? It must therefore be very foolish to condemn a thing you have not experienced either in your own person or in anyone else's. Why do you complain of me and of destiny? Do we do you any wrong? Is it for you to govern us, or for us to govern you? Though, perhaps, your years are not complete, yet your life is. A man of small stature is as much a man as a giant; neither men nor their lives are measured by the yard. Chiron refused to be immortal when he was told the conditions on which he was to enjoy it, by the god of time itself and its duration, his father Saturn. Just seriously consider how much more unbearable and painful an immortal life would be to man than the life I have already given him. If you had not death, you would eternally curse me for having deprived you of it. I have mixed a little bitterness with it, so that, seeing how convenient it is, you might not too greedily and indiscreetly seek to embrace it; and so that you might be so settled in this moderation as neither to be sick of life nor to have any antipathy for dying — which I have decreed you shall do once — I have tempered both of them between pleasure and pain. It was I that taught Thales, the most eminent of your sages, that to live and to die were indifferent, which made him, very wisely, answer the man who asked him why he did not die: 'Because,' he said, 'it is indifferent.' Water, earth, air, and fire, and the other parts of this creation of mine, are no more instruments of your life than they are of your death. Why do you fear your last day? It contributes no more to your dissolution than every one of the rest. The last step is not the cause of your weariness; it only declares it. Every day travels towards death; the last only arrives at it." These are the good lessons our mother Nature teaches.

The most eminent of the sages, whom Nature taught that living and dying were indifferent — which is why, asked why then he did not die, he answered: because it is indifferent.

## modern-en · simon-thomas · 20:2

I am one of those who feel the power of imagination most strongly. Everyone is jostled by it, but some are knocked flat by it. It makes a very piercing impression upon me, and I make it my business to dodge it, lacking the strength to resist. I could live by the help of cheerful, healthy company alone: the mere sight of another's pain materially pains me, and I often take over the sensations of another person. A perpetual cough in someone else tickles my own lungs and throat. I more unwillingly visit a sick person I love and owe duty to than one I am indifferent about. I take possession of the disease I am concerned with, and lodge it in myself. I am not at all surprised that fancy gives fevers and sometimes kills people who give it too much scope and are too willing to indulge it. Simon Thomas was a great physician of his day. I remember meeting him one day at Toulouse at the house of a rich old fellow troubled with weak lungs. Discussing the method of cure with the patient, the doctor told him that one helpful thing would be for him to give me reason to enjoy his company so much that I would come often to see him; by which means, fixing his eyes on my fresh complexion and his imagination on the sprightliness and vigour glowing in my youth, and filling all his senses with the flourishing age I was then in, his bodily state might perhaps be improved. But the doctor forgot to say that mine, at the same time, might be made worse. Gallus Vibius bent his mind so hard on finding out the essence and motions of madness that, in the end, he went out of his wits himself, and to such a degree that he could never afterwards recover his judgment, and might boast he had become a fool by too much wisdom. Some men, through fear, anticipate the hangman; and there was the man whose eyes, on being unbound to have his pardon read to him, were found stark dead upon the scaffold, by the stroke of imagination alone. We start, tremble, turn pale, and blush as we are variously moved by imagination; and lying in bed we feel our bodies stirred by its power to such a degree that we sometimes expire from it. And boiling youth, when fast asleep, grows so heated with fancy as in a dream to satisfy amorous desires:

A great physician of his time, who prescribed Montaigne’s company to a rich old man with weak lungs — that the sight of a fresh complexion and the imagination of a young man’s vigour might mend his habit of body. He forgot to add, Montaigne says, that it might make the young man’s worse.

## modern-en · cippus · 20:4

Though it is no new thing to see horns grown overnight on the forehead of one who had none when he went to bed, what happened to Cippus, King of Italy, is memorable: having one day been a delighted spectator at a bullfight, and having dreamed all night that he had horns on his head, he actually, by force of imagination, caused them to grow there. Passion gave to the son of Croesus the voice that nature had denied him. And Antiochus fell into a fever, inflamed by the beauty of Stratonice too deeply imprinted in his soul. Pliny claims to have seen Lucius Cossitius, who from a woman was turned into a man on her wedding day itself. Pontanus and others report a similar metamorphosis happening in our own time in Italy. And, through the vehement desire of himself and his mother:

Who watched a bullfight with delight, dreamed all night that he had horns on his head, and by the force of imagination grew them.

## modern-en · iphis · 20:5

"Volta puer solvit, quae foemina voverat, Iphis."

The boy who paid the vow a girl had made, in the line Montaigne quotes for such changes.

## modern-en · mary-germain · 20:6

I myself, passing through Vitry-le-François, saw a man whom the Bishop of Soissons had named Germain in confirmation, whom all the inhabitants of the place had known to be a girl until the age of twenty-two, called Mary. He was, at the time I was there, full of beard, old, and unmarried. He told us that by straining himself in a leap his male organs had come out; and the girls of the place have, to this day, a song warning each other not to take too great strides, for fear of being turned into men, as Mary Germain was. It is no wonder if this kind of accident happens often; for if imagination has any power in such matters, the desire is so continually and vigorously fixed on this subject that, in order not to relapse into the same thought and violence of longing, it would be better, once for all, to give these young girls the things they crave.

The man the Bishop of Soissons confirmed as Germain, whom the whole town had known as a girl called Mary until she was twenty-two. Montaigne saw him at Vitry le François, full of beard, old and unmarried; he said his male organs came out when he strained himself in a leap, and the girls of the place still have a song warning each other against taking too great strides.

## modern-en · dagobert · 20:7

Some attribute the scars of King Dagobert and of St. Francis to the force of imagination. It is said that by it bodies will sometimes be moved from their places; and Celsus tells us of a priest whose soul would be carried away into such an ecstasy that his body would for a long time remain without sense or breath. St. Augustine mentions another who, on hearing any lamentable or doleful cries, would immediately fall into a swoon and be so far out of himself that it was no use calling out, bawling in his ears, pinching or burning him until he voluntarily came to. Then he would say he had heard voices as it were far off, and had felt them pinching and burning him; and, to show that this was no obstinate dissimulation in defiance of his sense of feeling, it was clear that all the while he had neither pulse nor breath.

Whose scars, with St Francis’s, some attribute to the force of imagination.

## modern-en · jacques-pelletier · 20:10

A Count of a very great family, with whom I was very intimate, had married a fair lady who had previously been courted by a man present at the wedding. All his friends were in great fear — especially an old lady, a kinswoman of his, who was directing the ceremony and in whose house it was held — that the rival would play foul with sorcery. She told me her fear. I told her to leave it to me. I happened to have on me a flat plate of gold engraved with certain celestial figures, supposed to be good against sunstroke or headache when applied to the suture; to keep it firm, it was sewed to a ribbon to be tied under the chin — a piece of nonsense closely related to the one I am speaking of. Jacques Pelletier, who lived in my house, had presented it to me as a rare curiosity. I had a fancy to make some use of this trinket, and so privately told the Count that he might possibly run the same fortune other bridegrooms had run, especially since someone was in the house who would no doubt be glad to do him such a service. But let him go boldly to bed: I would do him the office of a friend, and, if needed, would not spare a miracle in my power, provided he would swear on his honour to keep it to himself; only, when they came to bring him his caudle, if matters had not gone well, to give me such-and-such a sign, and leave the rest to me. Now his ears had been so battered, and his mind so prepossessed with the endless chatter on the subject, that when the moment came, he did indeed find himself tied by the trouble of his imagination, and at the appointed time gave me the sign. Whereupon I whispered in his ear that he should get up, on the pretence of putting us out of the room, and in a jesting way pull my nightgown off my shoulders — we were of much the same height — throw it over his own, and keep it on him until he had carried out what I directed: which was that, when we had all gone out of the chamber, he should withdraw to make water, repeat three times such and such words and as often perform such and such actions; that each of the three times he should tie the ribbon I had put into his hand around his waist, and be sure to place the medal fastened to it, with the figures in a certain posture, exactly upon his loins; and that, having on the last of the three times tied the ribbon so tight that it could neither untie nor slip, he should confidently return to his business — and, by the way, not forget to spread my gown over the bed so that it would be sure to cover them both. These monkey-tricks are the main of the effect: our fancy is so easily seduced as to believe that such strange means must necessarily come from some abstruse science; their very emptiness gives them weight and reverence. And it is certain that my figures showed themselves to be more venereal than solar, more active than prohibitive. It was a sudden whim, mixed with a little curiosity, that made me do something so contrary to my nature: for I am an enemy to all subtle and counterfeit actions, and detest all kinds of trickery, though for sport and to someone's benefit; for though the action may not be vicious in itself, the manner is.

Who lived in Montaigne’s house and gave him the graven gold plate against sunstroke that Montaigne then used, with some private instructions and his own nightgown, to cure a bridegroom of an imagined impotence.

## modern-en · amasis · 20:11

Amasis, King of Egypt, having married Laodice, a very beautiful Greek virgin, although otherwise known for his abilities, found himself quite another man with his wife and could not by any means enjoy her; at which he was so enraged that he threatened to kill her, suspecting her of witchcraft. As is usual in matters of fancy, she put him onto devotion; and having made his vows to Venus, he found himself divinely restored the very first night after his offerings and sacrifices. Now women are to blame to greet us with that disdainful, coy, and angry face which extinguishes our vigour even as it kindles our desire — which made the daughter-in-law of Pythagoras say that "the woman who goes to bed with a man must put off her modesty with her petticoat, and put it back on with it." The soul of the assailant, agitated by many alarms at once, readily loses the power of performance; and once the imagination has played this trick on a man and shamed him (which it never does except at the first encounter, since men are then more ardent and eager, and at this first account a man gives of himself, he is much more afraid of failing), having made a bad start, he falls into such fever and vexation at the accident that it tends to remain with him on subsequent occasions.

Who married the beautiful Greek Laodice, found himself quite another man with her, threatened to kill her for a witch — and after vows to Venus was divinely restored the first night after his sacrifices.

## modern-en · laodice · 20:11

Amasis, King of Egypt, having married Laodice, a very beautiful Greek virgin, although otherwise known for his abilities, found himself quite another man with his wife and could not by any means enjoy her; at which he was so enraged that he threatened to kill her, suspecting her of witchcraft. As is usual in matters of fancy, she put him onto devotion; and having made his vows to Venus, he found himself divinely restored the very first night after his offerings and sacrifices. Now women are to blame to greet us with that disdainful, coy, and angry face which extinguishes our vigour even as it kindles our desire — which made the daughter-in-law of Pythagoras say that "the woman who goes to bed with a man must put off her modesty with her petticoat, and put it back on with it." The Greek virgin Amasis married and at first could not enjoy.

## modern-en · vives · 20:13

The unruly freedom of this member is very remarkable — so importunately ungovernable in its swelling and impatience when we have no need of it, and so unseasonably disobedient when we stand most in need of it; so imperiously contending in authority with the will, and with such haughty obstinacy refusing all our solicitation, both of hand and mind. And yet, though his rebellion is so universally complained of, and proof drawn from it to condemn him, if he had retained me to plead his cause, I should perhaps bring the rest of his fellow members under suspicion of conspiring against him out of pure envy at the importance and pleasure peculiar to his employment, and of having, by collusion, armed the whole world against him by maliciously charging him alone with their common offence. For let anyone consider whether there is any one part of our bodies that does not often refuse to perform its office at the will's command, and that does not often exercise its function in defiance of her command. Every one of them has passions of its own, that rouse and waken, stupefy and benumb them, without our consent. How often do involuntary movements of the face betray our inward thoughts, and disclose our most private secrets to bystanders. The same cause that animates this member also, without our knowledge, animates the lungs, the pulse, and the heart, the sight of a pleasing object imperceptibly spreading a flame through all our parts with a feverish motion. Is it only these veins and muscles that swell and slacken without the consent not only of the will but even of our knowledge? We do not command our hairs to stand on end, nor our skin to shiver with fear or desire; the hands often go to parts we do not direct them to; the tongue will be tied and the voice frozen when we do not know how to help it. When we have nothing to eat and would willingly suppress it, the appetite does not on that account stop stirring up the parts that are subject to it, no more, no less, than the other appetite we were speaking of; and likewise unseasonably leaves us when it thinks fit. The vessels that serve to discharge the belly have their own dilations and compressions, without and beyond our concurrence, just as do those that serve to purge the kidneys; and what St. Augustine offers, to vindicate the prerogative of the will, of having seen a man who could on command release his rear as often as he pleased, Vives, his commentator, further reinforces with another example from his own day — of a man who could break wind in tune. But these cases do not prove any more pure obedience in that part; for is anything commonly more tumultuous or indiscreet? To which let me add that I myself knew a man so rude and ungovernable that for forty years together he made his master vent with one continuous, uninterrupted outburst, and it is likely will do so till he dies of it. And I could heartily wish I only knew by reading how often a man's belly, by holding back a single puff, brings him to the very door of an exceedingly painful death; and that the emperor who gave free leave to break wind anywhere had at the same time given us the power to do it. But for our will, on whose behalf we are prosecuting, with how much greater probability may we reproach her herself with mutiny and sedition, for her irregularity and disobedience? Does she always will what we would have her will? Does she not often will what we forbid her to will, and to our manifest harm? Does she suffer herself, any more than the rest, to be governed and directed by the conclusions of our reason? To conclude, I should move, in defence of the gentleman my client, that in this matter his cause being inseparably and indistinctly joined with an accomplice, he alone is called in question, and that by arguments and accusations which cannot be charged on the other — whose business it is sometimes inopportunely to invite, but never to refuse, and to invite, moreover, in a tacit and quiet manner; and therefore the malice and injustice of his accusers are most plainly apparent. But however that may be, with my protest against the proceedings of the advocates and judges, nature will meanwhile proceed after her own way — who would have done well had she endowed this member with some particular privilege: the author of the sole immortal work of mortals; a divine work, according to Socrates; and love, the desire of immortality, and himself an immortal demon.

St Augustine’s commentator, who fortified his master’s example of the man who could command his rear with another, of a man who could break wind in tune.

## modern-en · charles-iv · 20:22

Magicians are no very good authority with me. But we have experimentally seen that women impart the marks of their fancy to the children they carry in the womb: witness the woman who was brought to bed of a Moor; and there was presented to Charles, the Emperor and King of Bohemia, a girl from near Pisa, all over rough and covered with hair, who her mother said was so conceived because of a picture of St. John the Baptist hanging within the curtains of her bed.

To whom a girl from near Pisa was presented, rough and covered with hair all over — conceived so, her mother said, because a picture of St John the Baptist hung inside her bed-curtains.

## modern-en · john-the-baptist · 20:22

Magicians are no very good authority with me. But we have experimentally seen that women impart the marks of their fancy to the children they carry in the womb: witness the woman who was brought to bed of a Moor; and there was presented to Charles, the Emperor and King of Bohemia, a girl from near Pisa, all over rough and covered with hair, who her mother said was so conceived because of a picture of St. John the Baptist hanging within the curtains of her bed.

Whose picture, hanging in the bed-curtains, the mother blamed for her hairy daughter.

## modern-en · jacob · 20:23

It is the same with beasts: witness Jacob's sheep, and the hares and partridges that the snow turns white on the mountains. There was at my house, a little while ago, a cat seen watching a bird at the top of a tree: after they had for some time fixed their eyes on each other, the bird at last let herself fall, dead, into the cat's claws — either dazzled by the force of her own imagination, or drawn by some attractive power of the cat. Those addicted to the pleasures of the field have no doubt heard the story of the falconer who, having earnestly fixed his eyes on a kite in the air, laid a wager that he could bring her down by the sole power of his sight, and did so, as it was said — for the tales I borrow I lay upon the consciences of those from whom I have them. The reasonings are mine, and rest on the proofs of reason, not of experience; to which everyone is at liberty to add his own examples. Whoever has none, let him not refuse, given the number and variety of accidents, to believe that there are plenty of them; if I do not apply them well, let someone else do so for me. Likewise, in the subject I am treating — our manners and motions — testimonies and instances, however fabulous, serve as well as the true, provided they are possible; whether they really happened or not, at Rome or Paris, to John or Peter, they are still within the range of human capacity, which is enough for me. I see and make my advantage of it, in shadow as well as in substance; and among the various readings of history, I cull out the most rare and memorable to fit my own turn. There are authors whose sole aim and design is to give an account of things that have happened; mine, if I could reach it, would be to deliver what may happen. There is a just liberty in the schools, of supposing similitudes when they have none at hand. I do not, however, make any use of that privilege, and in this matter, by a religious scrupulousness, I surpass all historical authority. In the examples I bring in of what I have heard, read, done, or said, I have forbidden myself to dare to alter even the most trivial and indifferent circumstances; my conscience does not falsify a syllable; what my ignorance may do, I cannot say.

Whose sheep are Montaigne’s scriptural instance that a mother’s fancy marks what she carries.

## modern-en · sallust · 20:25

I am pressed to write the affairs of my own time by some who fancy I look on them with an eye less blinded by passion than another, and have a clearer insight into them because of the free access fortune has given me to the heads of various factions; but they do not consider that, to gain the glory of Sallust, I would not give myself the trouble — sworn enemy as I am to obligation, assiduity, and perseverance; that there is nothing so contrary to my style as a continued narrative, since I so often interrupt and cut myself short in my writing for want of breath; I have neither composition nor explanation worth anything, and I am ignorant, beyond a child, of the phrases and even the very words proper to express the most common things. And for that reason I have undertaken to say only what I can say, and have fitted my subject to my strength. Should I take someone for a guide, perhaps I would not be able to keep pace with him; and in the freedom of my liberty might deliver judgments which, on second thought and by reason's standard, would be illegitimate and punishable. Plutarch would say of what he has handed down to us, that it is the work of others — that his examples are entirely and everywhere exactly true; that they are useful to posterity and are presented with a lustre that will light the way to virtue, is his own work. It is not of such dangerous consequence, as in a medicinal drug, whether an old story be so or otherwise.

The Roman historian, whose glory Montaigne says he would not take the trouble to earn.
